The **Maduro regime**[^cite_note-4] refers to the period in Venezuelan history from 2013 to 2026, when Venezuela was ruled by [Nicolás Maduro](7161476.md)'s [authoritarian regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarianism). The period began with Maduro's assumption of the presidency on 14 April 2013, and formally came to an end with the [U.S. intervention in Venezuela](82021123.md), on 3 January 2026. While Maduro initially operated in a [hybrid regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hybrid_regime), Venezuela turned into a full dictatorship following the [2017 constitutional crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Venezuelan_constitutional_crisis).

The **second presidency of Nicolás Maduro** (2019–2025)[^cite_note-5] represents the fifth governmental period in Venezuela under the [Bolivarian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolivarian_Revolution). It began amidst a 68% voter abstention rate[^cite_note-6] and concluded with allegations of electoral fraud, with Maduro assuming an early start to what would be his third term.[^cite_note-\:18000-7]

The foreign policy of Maduro's second term was characterized by the severance of diplomatic relations with [Argentina](18951905.md), [Bolivia](3462.md) (restored in 2020),[^cite_note-\:5-8] [Brazil](3383.md) (restored in 2022),[^cite_note-\:3-9] [Colombia](5222.md) (restored in 2022),[^cite_note-\:6-10] [El Salvador](9356.md), the [United States](3434750.md),[^cite_note-\:7-11] [Guatemala](17238567.md)[^cite_note-\:2-12] and [Paraguay](23420.md). As well as a [territorial dispute with Guyana](6896458.md) over the Essequibo region. In March 2024, Venezuela passed a law that designates Essequibo as a new state of Venezuela, governed from the city of [Tumeremo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tumeremo). The law was submitted to the Supreme Court to validate its constitutionality.[^cite_note-13][^cite_note-14]

Following the growing geopolitical and electoral tensions of 2024 and 2025, the United States' [Trump administration](78298108.md) began preparing an intervention against Nicolás Maduro's regime, officially accusing the Venezuelan government of running a narco-terrorist network and conspiring to flood the United States with cocaine.[^cite_note-15] On 3 January 2026, Venezuela was targeted by a swift U.S. military raid, dubbed Operation Absolute Resolve, which bombed the country's air defense infrastructure and removed Maduro from power within three hours. During this raid, American troops captured Maduro alongside his wife, [Cilia Flores](9530834.md), at their Caracas residence and immediately extradited them to New York. Followed by the capture of [Alex Saab](61390840.md), in February 2026. Starting in March 2026, Maduro was [put on trial](82024138.md) before the U.S. Federal Court in the Southern District of New York to face charges of drug trafficking, criminal conspiracy, and possession of heavy weapons.[^cite_note-nyt2020-16][^cite_note-npr2020-17]

## History

On 14 April 2013, Nicolás Maduro was [elected President of Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Venezuelan_presidential_election), narrowly defeating opposition candidate [Henrique Capriles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henrique_Capriles) with just a popular vote lead of 1.5%. Capriles immediately demanded a recount, refusing to recognize the outcome as valid.[^cite_note-18] Maduro was later formally inaugurated as president on 19 April, after the election commission had promised a full audit of the election results.[^cite_note-sworn-19][^cite_note-20] On 24 October 2013, he announced the creation of a new agency, the Vice Ministry of Supreme Happiness, to coordinate all the social programmes.[^cite_note-21]

Beginning six months after being elected, Maduro [ruled by decree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rule_by_decree) for the majority of his presidency: from 19 November 2013 to 19 November 2014,[^cite_note-WPdecree-22] 15 March 2015 to 31 December 2015, 15 January 2016 to present.[^cite_note-DECREE8-23]

### 2013–2014

In October 2013, Maduro requested an enabling law to [rule by decree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rule_by_decree) in order to fight corruption[^cite_note-24][^cite_note-25] and to also fight what he called an "economic war" which had the goal of radically reducing poverty.[^cite_note-26] Implementing price controls, wage controls, profit controls, and general economic restructuring. On 19 November 2013, the National Assembly granted Maduro the power to rule by decree until 19 November 2014.[^cite_note-27]

### 2015–2016

On 10 March 2015, Maduro asked to rule by decree for a second time following the sanctioning of seven Venezuelan officials by the United States, requesting the Enabling Law to be used to "confront" what Maduro called "the aggression of the most powerful country in the world, the United States".[^cite_note-28] Days later on 15 March 2015, the National Assembly granted Maduro power to rule by decree until 31 December 2015.[^cite_note-BBCdecree-29]

### 2016–2017

After a coalition of opposition parties won in the [6 December 2015 elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Venezuelan_parliamentary_election), the [lame duck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lame_duck_\(politics\)) Assembly named 13 new Justices sympathetic toward Maduro to the [Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Venezuela).[^cite_note-30] On 15 January 2016, Maduro declared an economic emergency and issued a "vaguely worded" decree that would grant himself extraordinary powers for 60 days, or until 15 March 2016.[^cite_note-GUARDdecree-31][^cite_note-32] Days after on 18 March 2016, the expiration of the decree powers, the Supreme Court granted Maduro the power to rule by decree for an additional 60 days, or until 17 May 2016.[^cite_note-INDdecree-33]

Days before his second 60-day rule by decree were to end, Maduro stated on 11 May 2016 that he would continue to rule by decree through the rest of the year until 2017.[^cite_note-34]

### 2017–2018

While meeting with the Supreme Tribunal of Justice on 15 January 2017, Maduro signed a new economic decree, extending his rule by decree for the sixth time since the original ruling in January 2016.[^cite_note-35] On 19 January, the Supreme Tribunal of Justice established the "Decree on the State of Emergency and Economic Emergency", granting Maduro to rule by decree further into 2017.[^cite_note-36]

On 13 May 2017 at a time of rising unrest during the [2017 Venezuelan protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Venezuelan_protests), President Maduro extended his decree powers for the eighth time since January 2016, allowing him to rule by decree for another 60 days.[^cite_note-DECREE8-23] The powers were extended again on 13 July 2017 for an additional 60 days.[^cite_note-37]

On 15 October, the Bolivarian government [Great Patriotic Pole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Patriotic_Pole) won 18 of the 23 governorships while the opposition only 5 during the [2017 Venezuelan regional elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Venezuelan_regional_elections).

On 10 December, the Bolivarian government [Great Patriotic Pole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Patriotic_Pole) won 306 of the 337 Mayorships during the [2017 Venezuelan municipal elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Venezuelan_municipal_elections).

### Cabinet of Maduro

Main article: [Cabinet of Nicolás Maduro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_of_Nicol%C3%A1s_Maduro)

### Cult of personality

See also: [List of cults of personality § Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cults_of_personality#Venezuela)



A mural of Nicolás Maduro, saying "For the love of Chávez. Maduro (for) President." with the popular "Chávez eyes" visible.

Just like his predecessor, Maduro maintained a cult of personality, framing himself as the "Son of Chávez," dominating public media with heroic iconography, such as the *[Súper Bigote y su mano de hierro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%BAper_Bigote_y_su_mano_de_hierro)* cartoon,[^cite_note-\:2-12][^cite_note-\:3-9] and flooding urban landscapes with official murals and billboards. Following Maduro's ousting during the 2026 U.S. intervention, Maduro's cult of personality was systematically erased by authorities.[^cite_note-38]

### Military authority

> Since coming to power three years ago, Mr. Maduro has relied increasingly on the armed forces as a spiraling economic crisis pushed his approval ratings to record lows and [food shortages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shortages_in_Venezuela) led to lootings. ... The armed forces have swiftly repressed all opposition rallies as well as the food riots that flare up daily across the country.

— *[The Wall Street Journal](173070.md)*

[^cite_note-WSJpadrino-39]

Maduro relied on the military to maintain power since he was initially elected into office.[^cite_note-WSJpadrino-39] According to Luis Manuel Esculpi, a Venezuelan security analyst, "The army is Maduro's only source of authority."[^cite_note-WSJpadrino-39] As time passed, Maduro grew more reliant on the military, showing that Maduro was losing power as described by [Amherst College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amherst_College) professor, Javier Corrales.[^cite_note-DWpadrino-40] Corrales explains that "From 2003 until Chavez died in 2013, the civilian wing was strong, so he did not have to fall back on the military. As civilians withdrew their support, Maduro was forced to resort to military force."[^cite_note-DWpadrino-40] *[The New York Times](30680.md)* stated that Maduro no longer had the oil revenue to buy loyalty for protection, instead relying on favorable exchange rates, as well as the smuggling of food and drugs, which "also generate revenue".[^cite_note-41]



President Maduro among troops during a May 2016 exercise

On 12 July 2016, Maduro granted Defense Minister [Vladimir Padrino López](45272341.md) the power to oversee product transportation, price controls, the [Bolivarian missions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolivarian_missions), while also having his military command five of Venezuela's main ports.[^cite_note-42][^cite_note-PAPpower-43][^cite_note-LAHTpadrino-44] This action performed by President Maduro made General Padrino one of the most powerful people in Venezuela, possibly "the second most powerful man in Venezuelan politics".[^cite_note-PAPpower-43][^cite_note-BLOOMcomandante-45] The appointment of Padrino was also seen to be similar to the Cuban government's tactic of granting the Cuban military the power to manage Cuba's economy.[^cite_note-PAPpower-43] It is the first time since the dictatorship of General [Marcos Perez Jimenez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcos_Perez_Jimenez) in 1958 that a military official has held such power in Venezuela.[^cite_note-LAHTpadrino-44] According to Corrales, "For all of the ministers of the cabinet to have to respond to a soldier, this is associated with [military dictatorships](146728.md)".[^cite_note-WSJpadrino-39]

According to Nicolás Maduro:[^cite_note-PAPpower-43]

> All ministries and government institutions are subordinated to the National Command of the Great Mission for Safe Sovereign and Safe Supply, which is under the command of the President and of the top General, [Vladimir Padrino López](45272341.md).

### Domestic policy



[UNASUR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_South_American_Nations) special meeting to discuss the [diversion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evo_Morales_grounding_incident) of Bolivian President [Evo Morales](284163.md)' plane in Europe, 4 July 2013

Maduro said that Venezuela was not facing a [humanitarian crisis](52966258.md).[^cite_note-46] Maduro stuck to his predecessor Hugo Chávez's policies in order to remain popular to those who find a connection between the two. Despite the increasingly difficult crises facing Venezuela, such as a faltering economy and high crime rate, Maduro continued the use of Chávez's policies.[^cite_note-47]

After continuing Chávez's policies, Maduro's support among Venezuelans began to decrease, with [*Bloomberg*](432006.md) explaining that he held on to power by placing opponents in jail and impeding upon Venezuela's freedom of press.[^cite_note-48] According to [Marsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marsh_\(company\)), instead of making any policy changes, Maduro placed attention on his "hold on power by closing off the legal channels through which the opposition can act".[^cite_note-MARSH2017-49] Shannon K. O'Neil of the [Council on Foreign Relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_on_Foreign_Relations) stated that "After Chavez's death, Maduro has just continued and accelerated the authoritarian and totalitarian policies of Chavez".[^cite_note-50]



The rally against Maduro's government in October 2016



The rally in support of Maduro's government in December 2016

Regarding Maduro's ideology, Professor Ramón Piñango, a sociologist from the Venezuelan University of IESA, "Maduro has a very strong ideological orientation, close to the Communist ideology. Contrary to Diosdado, he is not very pragmatic".[^cite_note-aljazeera-51] Maduro himself stated that Venezuela must build a more socialist nation, highlighting that the country needs an economic overhaul, a political-military union and government involvement in the workplace.[^cite_note-52]

### Crime

Further information: [Crime in Venezuela](37476256.md)

One of the first important presidential programs of Maduro became the "Safe Homeland" program, a massive police and military campaign to build security in the country. Three thousand soldiers were deployed to decrease homicide in Venezuela, which has one of the highest rates of homicide in Latin America.[^cite_note-bbc.co.uk-53] Most of these troops were deployed in the state of Miranda (Greater Caracas), which has the highest homicide rate in Venezuela. According to the government, in 2012, more than 16,000 people were killed, a rate of 54 people per 100,000, although the Venezuela Violence Observatory, a Venezuelan NGO, state that the homicide rate was 73 people per 100,000.[^cite_note-bbc.co.uk-53] The program had to be reinitiated one year later after the program's creator, [Miguel Rodríguez Torres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_Rodr%C3%ADguez_Torres), was replaced by [Carmen Melendez Teresa Rivas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carmen_Melendez_Teresa_Rivas). Murder also increased over the years since the program's initiation according to the Venezuela Violence Observatory, with the murder rate increasing to 82 per 100,000 in 2014.[^cite_note-54] 23,047 homicides were committed in Venezuela in 2018, a rate of 81.4 per 100,000 people.[^cite_note-55]

### Economic

Main article: [Economic policy of the Nicolás Maduro government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_policy_of_the_Nicol%C3%A1s_Maduro_government)

When elected in 2013, Maduro continued the majority of [existing economic policies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_policy_of_the_Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez_administration) of his predecessor [Hugo Chávez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez). When entering the presidency, Maduro's Venezuela faced a high inflation rate and [large shortages of goods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shortages_in_Venezuela)[^cite_note-economist.com-56][^cite_note-April2014-57][^cite_note-qz.com-58] that was left over from the previous administration of President Chávez.[^cite_note-unstable-59][^cite_note-FPmarch2013-60][^cite_note-NPRdec2014-61][^cite_note-ELPAISfeb2015-62]

Maduro blamed wealthy businessmen for hoarding goods and speculation that is driving high rates of inflation and creating widespread shortages of staples, and often said he was fighting an "economic war", calling newly enacted economic measures "economic offensives" against political opponents he and loyalists state are behind an international economic conspiracy.[^cite_note-NYTjan2015-63][^cite_note-64][^cite_note-econOFF-65][^cite_note-LNecon-66][^cite_note-Edition.cnn.com-67][^cite_note-ENeconoffense-68] However, Maduro was criticized for only concentrating on public opinion instead of tending to the practical issues economists have warned the Venezuelan government about or creating any ideas to improve the economic situation in Venezuela such as the "economic war".[^cite_note-TNYnov2014-69][^cite_note-70]

Venezuela was ranked as the top spot globally with the highest [misery index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misery_index_\(economics\)) score in 2013,[^cite_note-71] 2014,[^cite_note-72] 2015,[^cite_note-73][^cite_note-BLOOMmarzo2-74] and 2016.[^cite_note-75] In 2014, Venezuela's economy entered an [economic depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_depression)[^cite_note-76] that continued as of 2017.[^cite_note-MARSH2017-49] Under Maduro's rule, [GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GDP) approximately halved.[^cite_note-veconomist-77]

### Defense

In 2023, Nicolás Maduro conducted a significant reshuffling of the Venezuelan military high command, retiring the general commanders of all branches: the [Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolivarian_Army_of_Venezuela), [Military Aviation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolivarian_Military_Aviation_of_Venezuela), [National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolivarian_National_Guard_of_Venezuela), and [Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolivarian_Navy_of_Venezuela), as well as the commanders of the civilian-military [Bolivarian Militia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolivarian_Militia), the Integrated Aerospace Defense Command (Codai), and the Inspector General of the Armed Forces.[^cite_note-78]

In May 2024, it was announced that one million members of the [Bolivarian Militia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolivarian_Militia) would undergo weapons training.[^cite_note-79]

### Media policy

In 2019, several international news channels were taken off the air in Venezuela, including [Canal 24 Horas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/24_Horas_\(Spanish_TV_channel\)), [TV Chile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TV_Chile), [Antena 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antena_3_\(Spanish_TV_channel\)), [National Geographic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Geographic_\(American_TV_channel\)), [CNN](62028.md), [BBC](19344654.md), Telearuba, and TeleCuraçao. Between 2021 and 2022, the National Commission of Telecommunications ([Conatel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Commission_of_Telecommunications)) blocked 45 news websites. The [Inter American Press Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inter_American_Press_Association) condemned these actions as a "gag on independent journalism."[^cite_note-80][^cite_note-81] In 2023, TBS and TruTV also ceased broadcasting in the country. Following a report on corruption in Venezuela, the German broadcaster [Deutsche Welle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deutsche_Welle) was taken off the air in March 2024, with President Maduro criticizing the outlet as "Nazi."[^cite_note-82]

According to the Chapultepec Index by the [Inter American Press Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inter_American_Press_Association), Venezuela was ranked last (#22) in the index of freedom of expression and the press in the Americas for both 2020 and 2021, receiving the classification of a country "without freedom of expression." The country rose to the #21 position in 2022 and remained there in 2023, still with the classification of "without freedom of expression."[^cite_note-83][^cite_note-84]

### Military

> Since coming to power three years ago, Mr. Maduro has relied increasingly on the armed forces as a spiraling economic crisis pushed his approval ratings to record lows and [food shortages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shortages_in_Venezuela) led to lootings. ... The armed forces have swiftly repressed all opposition rallies as well as the food riots that flare up daily across the country.

— *[The Wall Street Journal](173070.md)*

[^cite_note-WSJpadrino-39]

Maduro relied on the military to maintain power since he was initially elected into office.[^cite_note-WSJpadrino-39] He promised to make Venezuela a [great power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_power) by 2050, stating that the Venezuelan military would lead the way to make the country "a powerhouse, of happiness, of equality".[^cite_note-85]

On 12 July 2016, Maduro granted Defense Minister [Vladimir Padrino López](45272341.md) the power to oversee product transportation, price controls, the [Bolivarian missions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolivarian_missions), while also having his military command five of Venezuela's main ports.[^cite_note-86][^cite_note-PAPpower3-87][^cite_note-LAHTpadrino2-88] This action performed by President Maduro made General Padrino one of the most powerful people in Venezuela, possibly "the second most powerful man in Venezuelan politics".[^cite_note-BLOOMcomandante-45][^cite_note-PAPpower3-87] The appointment of Padrino was also seen to be similar to the Cuban government's tactic of granting the Cuban military the power to manage Cuba's economy.[^cite_note-PAPpower3-87]

&#x20;According to Nicolás Maduro:[^cite_note-PAPpower3-87]

> All ministries and government institutions are subordinated to the National Command of the Great Mission for Safe Sovereign and Safe Supply, which is under the command of the President and of the top General, [Vladimir Padrino López](45272341.md).

It was the first time since the dictatorship of General [Marcos Pérez Jiménez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcos_P%C3%A9rez_Jim%C3%A9nez) in 1958 that a military official has held such power in Venezuela.[^cite_note-LAHTpadrino2-88]

### Judicial policy

#### Appointment of magistrates

The [2015 Venezuelan parliamentary election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Venezuelan_parliamentary_election) was held on 6 December 2015.[^cite_note-89] The elections resulted in the victory of the [Democratic Unity Roundtable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Unity_Roundtable) (MUD), the main opposition movement to the government of President [Nicolás Maduro](7161476.md), with 112 of the 167 seats in the National Assembly (56.2% of the vote), and the first major electoral victory for the opposition in 17 years.[^cite_note-90]

In response to the opposition's victory, [Chavismo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chavismo) reacted with the appointment of 13 principal magistrates and 21 substitutes to the [Supreme Tribunal of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Tribunal_of_Justice_of_Venezuela) (TSJ), carried out on 23 December 2015 by the [National Assembly dominated by Chavistas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/III_Legislature_of_the_National_Assembly_of_Venezuela?action=edit\&redlink=1) in a record time of less than fifteen days.[^cite_note-91] Both the opposition and various legal scholars described the appointments as illegal because they were not carried out in accordance with the [Constitution of Venezuela](1211684.md) and the Organic Law of the Supreme Tribunal of Justice, including violations of the objection period, the lack of responses to objections, and the omission of the final selection of candidates, in addition to the fact that the appointments were made after the legislative year had ended on 15 December.[^cite_note-92]

#### Suspension of Amazonas deputies

On 5 January 2016, the constitutional term of the National Legislative Power began, with a total of 163 of the 167 deputies elected in December 2015, due to the suspension of the election in [Amazonas state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amazonas_\(Venezuelan_state\)) by the Venezuelan [Supreme Tribunal of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Tribunal_of_Justice_of_Venezuela) (TSJ), affecting four deputies.[^cite_note-93] On 6 January 2016, before the beginning of the first ordinary session, three of the four deputies whose elections had been suspended by the TSJ were sworn in by Assembly president [Henry Ramos Allup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Ramos_Allup), arguing that the court's decision was unconstitutional.[^cite_note-94] The remaining deputy, who belonged to the [United Socialist Party of Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Socialist_Party_of_Venezuela), decided not to be sworn in. Days later, the deputies decided to step down in order to end the supposed "contempt", with the Parliament approving the measure and notifying the TSJ of the decision on 13 January; however, the TSJ has not issued a ruling lifting the contempt sentence to this day.

#### Institutional crisis

Main articles: [2017 Venezuelan constitutional crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Venezuelan_constitutional_crisis) and [Supreme Tribunal of Justice of Venezuela in exile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Tribunal_of_Justice_of_Venezuela_in_exile)

As a result of the [2017 Venezuelan constitutional crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Venezuelan_constitutional_crisis), the [Public Ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Ministry_\(Venezuela\)?action=edit\&redlink=1) formally requested the annulment of the appointments, arguing that the process had been flawed.[^cite_note-95] Consequently, the National Assembly, now with an opposition majority, on 13 June 2017 swore in the Judicial Nominations Committee, chaired by deputy Carlos Berrizbeitia, for the election of new principal TSJ magistrates.[^cite_note-96] Although all the stages contemplated in the constitution and in the Organic Law of the Supreme Tribunal of Justice were completed, Venezuela's [Republican Moral Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Moral_Council) refused to carry out the preselection, arguing that the process was untimely.[^cite_note-97] Despite the council's refusal, on 21 July 2017 the National Assembly appointed 13 principal magistrates and 21 substitutes; by 24 July, three magistrates had been detained and 30 were in hiding.[^cite_note-98]

Attorney General [Luisa Ortega Díaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luisa_Ortega_D%C3%ADaz) criticized the appointments, arguing that they created disorder within the state.[^cite_note-99] For its part, the TSJ warned that the appointments were null under the pretext of the alleged contempt in which the National Assembly remained, while simultaneously requesting that civilian and military courts exercise the necessary measures of [coercion](6512.md).[^cite_note-100]

### Electoral policy

#### 2013 municipal elections

Main articles: [2013 Venezuelan municipal elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Venezuelan_municipal_elections) and [Dakazo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dakazo)

##### Dakazo



[Dakazo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dakazo).

The *Dakazo* was a series of actions taken by the Venezuelan government forcing [consumer electronics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consumer_electronics) stores, mainly Daka, to sell products at much lower prices on 8 November 2013, weeks before the [municipal elections that same year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Venezuelan_municipal_elections) and one month before [Christmas](6237.md). The price changes helped the ruling party, the [PSUV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PSUV), win some of the municipal elections, but also caused looting and product shortages in the following months.[^cite_note-101]

##### Municipal elections

The [municipal elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Venezuelan_municipal_elections) had originally been scheduled for Sunday, 14 April 2013, but due to the death of President [Hugo Chávez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez) and the announcement of new presidential elections for the same day, the [National Electoral Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Electoral_Council_\(Venezuela\)) decided to postpone them until 8 December 2013.[^cite_note-102] The results announced by the CNE indicated a victory for the [PSUV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Socialist_Party_of_Venezuela) and its allies in most municipalities of the country, obtaining 240 of the 337 mayoralties,[^cite_note-103] although the major cities were governed by opposition candidates. The ruling alliance also obtained the majority of municipal councils and the majority of votes nationwide, with 5,216,522 votes representing 48.69%, while the opposition alliance [MUD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Unity_Roundtable) obtained 4,373,910 votes representing 39.34%. The opposition won at least 81 mayoralties, an increase of 33.9% compared to the 56 mayoralties it had held four years earlier.[^cite_note-104]

#### Suspension of the presidential recall referendum

On 20 October 2016, the Venezuelan [National Electoral Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Electoral_Council_\(Venezuela\)) (CNE) suspended, until further notice, the collection of signatures corresponding to 20 percent of those registered in the national electoral registry required to request a recall referendum against the president's mandate.[^cite_note-105] This occurred because five different courts in the states of [Carabobo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carabobo), [Aragua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aragua), [Bolívar](556256.md), [Monagas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monagas) and [Apure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apure) simultaneously ruled to annul the [first signature collection process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016%E2%80%932017_Venezuelan_recall_referendum_petition_drive?action=edit\&redlink=1) for the referendum. On 21 October, the criminal court of [Zulia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zulia) state did the same.

##### Reactions

Opponents of the Maduro government considered the annulment a breakdown of Venezuela's constitutional order, and it was denounced by the Secretary General of the [Organization of American States](22788.md), since this popular vote was guaranteed and protected by the [1999 Constitution of Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1999_Constitution_of_Venezuela), established during the government of [Hugo Chávez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez).[^cite_note-106] Eleven OAS member states expressed concern over the suspension of the recall referendum in a public letter.[^cite_note-107]



Protests over the suspension of the referendum

Opponents also argued that the supposed constitutional breakdown was worsened by the adoption of a ruling by a *lower* court of *regional* jurisdiction as if it were valid nationwide. According to critics, this action would have been irregular because it went against standard Venezuelan legislative mechanisms, whereby only a court with national jurisdiction would be valid to modify or suspend a national referendum.[^cite_note-108]

The [2016 Venezuelan general strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Venezuelan_general_strike?action=edit\&redlink=1) was called for Friday, 28 October 2016, by several Venezuelan [trade union](17626.md) organizations, including [Democratic Action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Action_\(Venezuela\)), [Alianza Bravo Pueblo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alianza_Bravo_Pueblo), [Convergence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convergence_\(Venezuela\)), [Liberal Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Force), [Justice First](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justice_First), [A New Era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_New_Era), and movements such as the [Venezuelan student movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_student_movement?action=edit\&redlink=1), as a protest against the [suspension of the presidential recall referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Venezuelan_recall_referendum).

#### 2018 presidential election

Main article: [2018 Venezuelan presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Venezuelan_presidential_election)



Empty polling station in [Caracas](55289.md) during the 2018 elections.

The presidential elections in [Venezuela](32374.md) for the [2019–2025 term](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_presidency_of_Nicol%C3%A1s_Maduro) were held on Sunday, 20 May 2018. International organizations such as the [Office of the United Nations High Commissioner for Human Rights](22463.md), the [European Union](9317.md), the [Organization of American States](22788.md) and other countries rejected the election call due to its lack of transparency and electoral guarantees and stated that they would not recognize the results. However, countries such as [Russia](25391.md), [China](5405.md), [Turkey](11125639.md), [North Korea](21255.md), [Iran](14653.md), [Bolivia](3462.md), [Cuba](5042481.md), [El Salvador](9356.md) and [Nicaragua](21362.md) expressed support for the elections.[^cite_note-109]

The election, criticized for lacking transparency in the process of calling and conducting it, as well as for allegations concerning the use of political checkpoints near polling centers and the bias of the [electoral authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Electoral_Council_\(Venezuela\)), was internationally condemned. The contest registered the highest abstention rate in presidential election history since the return of democracy in 1958.[^cite_note-110]

Subsequently, at the beginning of January 2019, close to the inauguration date of reelected candidate Nicolás Maduro, a series of statements were issued expressing non-recognition of him as president, including by the [Organization of American States](22788.md), the [European Union](9317.md) and its member states.[^cite_note-111]

### Security policy

Main article: [Crime in Venezuela](37476256.md)

At the end of 2012, Venezuela's Minister of Interior and Justice, [Néstor Reverol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N%C3%A9stor_Reverol), said that murders had increased by 14% in 2012, reaching the figure of 16,072 homicides.[^cite_note-112] On 13 May 2013 the [Safe Homeland Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safe_Homeland_Plan?action=edit\&redlink=1) began, which involved the deployment of 3,000 troops of the [National Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Army_of_Venezuela) in [Caracas](55289.md) to assume functions of [public security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_security).[^cite_note-113]

On 22 September 2014, President Maduro announced that his government would invest \$47 million for the creation of 60 new disarmament centers, and \$39 million to finance the disarmament plan under which National Guard troops would patrol the most dangerous neighborhoods.[^cite_note-114]

On 13 July 2015 Maduro launched [Operation Liberation of the People](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Liberation_of_the_People?action=edit\&redlink=1) (OLP), a police deployment as a response to the increase of violence in [Venezuela](32374.md). It consists of operations where citizens are detained and verified in the Integrated Police Information System (SIIPOL) in order to determine whether any crime has been committed.[^cite_note-GV2-115] In April 2016 the [Special Actions Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Actions_Forces?action=edit\&redlink=1) (FAES), a command of the [Bolivarian National Police](28137819.md) (PNB), was created.[^cite_note-sit_well-116] The FAES have been accused of being a political instrument of President [Nicolás Maduro](7161476.md),[^cite_note-sit_well-116] as well as receiving accusations of being an [extermination group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_squad)[^cite_note-\:04-117] and [repression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_repression) of [opponents](75340539.md).[^cite_note-\:14000-118]

#### El Junquito massacre

Main article: [El Junquito raid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Junquito_raid)



Exact moment of the bombing against [Óscar Alberto Pérez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%93scar_Alberto_P%C3%A9rez) by the forces of the Bolivarian government.

On 15 January 2018 the [Operation Gideon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Gideon) was carried out in the El Junquito neighborhood, northwest of Caracas, where officials of the [CONAS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Anti-Extortion_and_Kidnapping_Command?action=edit\&redlink=1), [SEBIN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolivarian_Intelligence_Service), [DGCIM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Directorate_of_Military_Counterintelligence), [GNB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Guard_of_Venezuela), [FAES](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Actions_Forces?action=edit\&redlink=1), [PNB](28137819.md) and [Policaracas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polic%C3%ADa_de_Caracas?action=edit\&redlink=1)[^cite_note-119] stormed the [El Junquito Parish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Junquito_Parish,_Caracas?action=edit\&redlink=1) in search of Oscar Pérez.[^cite_note-120] Immediately afterwards a confrontation occurred, leaving several dead and wounded.[^cite_note-121]

Óscar Pérez died during the police operation.[^cite_note-122] In the operation ten people died: seven members of the opposition group, two officers of the [Bolivarian National Police Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolivarian_National_Police_Corps?action=edit\&redlink=1) and one leader of the so-called [colectivos](42254804.md). Various national and international movements and organizations categorized the action as a "massacre"[^cite_note-123] and/or "extrajudicial execution".[^cite_note-124]

After the death of Óscar Pérez, people close to him revealed that those responsible for the homicide had received the order not to take anyone alive and to kill Pérez. Shortly afterwards, Maduro confirmed that he had given the order to kill Óscar Pérez and threatened to apply the same tactic to any opposition group that he considered terrorist.

In the aftermath, Masonic lodges in Venezuela were raided. The death of Perez, a Free Mason, and subsequent government activities, were publicly condemned by lodges. It marked a history of Maduro's opposition to the fraternity.[^cite_note-125][^cite_note-126] Maduro had previously called Perez a terrorist.[^cite_note-127]

#### 2018 Caracas attack

Main article: [2018 Caracas drone attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Caracas_drone_attack)



[2018 Caracas attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Caracas_attack?action=edit\&redlink=1).

On 4 August 2018, in Caracas, Maduro was giving a speech at an event for the commemoration of the 81 years since the creation of the [Bolivarian National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolivarian_National_Guard) (GNB), when an explosion of a [drone](58900.md) loaded with the explosive [C-4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C-4_\(explosive\)) occurred.[^cite_note-128] The president was unharmed, but seven members of the GNB were reported injured.[^cite_note-129] As a consequence the [detention of Juan Requesents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detention_of_Juan_Requesens), deputy of the National Assembly, occurred, in addition to the arrest and assassination of opposition leader [Fernando Albán Salazar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernando_Alb%C3%A1n_Salazar).

### Transport policy

#### Crisis with airlines

Beginning in November 2013 some airlines began to have problems for the repatriation of capital to their main headquarters, after selling tickets in bolívares for reimbursement in dollars through the [Cadivi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cadivi?action=edit\&redlink=1) system.[^cite_note-130] The government maintains a debt with around 32 airlines since before 2014 in the time of CADIVI for a value of US\$3.5 billion, expressed the president of the Association of Airlines in Venezuela (Alav), Humberto Figuera[^cite_note-Sin_nombre-1_63F-1-131][^cite_note-132]

On 17 March 2014 [Air Canada](145623.md) announced that it suspended indefinitely its operations in Venezuela due to the lack of cancellation of the debt that the government maintained with the airline.[^cite_note-133] On 2 June [Alitalia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alitalia) suspended operations in Venezuela due to the multimillion-dollar debt of the government with the airline.[^cite_note-134] By November 2019 the debt to the airlines remained, year for which there were nine foreign airline companies firmly operating[^cite_note-135][^cite_note-Sin_nombre-1_63F-1-131]

### Odebrecht case in Venezuela

#### Allegations of bribery

The United States Department of Justice investigated the Brazilian construction company [Odebrecht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novonor) and determined that it had paid around 788 million dollars in bribes to 11 countries, apart from [Brazil](3383.md), beginning in 2001. Of this amount, Odebrecht allegedly gave around 98 million dollars to government officials and intermediaries in order to obtain State contracts. Odebrecht admitted responsibility and agreed to pay a fine of around 3.5 billion dollars.[^cite_note-136]

In Venezuela, 31 projects awarded to the Brazilian company without bidding became known under the alleged binational agreement (agreement between states) but which was carried out between the Venezuelan state and a private company from Brazil, as in the case of the [Tocoma Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tocoma_Dam)[^cite_note-137] when in February 2016 the external adviser of the Brazilian Odebrecht company and representative of the OIV Consortium could not satisfactorily explain to deputies of the [National Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Assembly_\(Venezuela\)) how the [Tocoma Hydroelectric Plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tocoma_Dam) project had four years of delay when he was questioned, for which political responsibility was declared for seven former electricity ministers: [Rafael Ramírez Carreño](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafael_Ram%C3%ADrez_Carre%C3%B1o); [Alí Rodríguez Araque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al%C3%AD_Rodr%C3%ADguez_Araque); [Jesse Chacón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesse_Chac%C3%B3n); [Argenis Chávez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argenis_Ch%C3%A1vez_Fr%C3%ADas); [Nervis Villalobos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nervis_Villalobos), and Javier Alvarado.[^cite_note-138]

#### Conviction of Marcelo Odebrecht in Brazil

On 7 March 2016, the former president of the company, [Marcelo Odebrecht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcelo_Odebrecht), was sentenced to 19 years and four months in prison in Brazil for having paid more than 30 million dollars in bribes to Petrobras executives in exchange for contracts and influence.[^cite_note-139]

#### Allegations of financing Chávez and Capriles campaigns

Between June and December 2017 it was alleged that the Odebrecht company had financed the campaigns of the two main presidential candidates of the [2012 elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Venezuelan_presidential_election): [Hugo Chávez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez) (the Brazilian publicist Mônica Moura was detained by the Brazilian justice system and declared that Chávez's campaign cost 35 million dollars and was illegally financed by [Odebrecht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odebrecht))[^cite_note-chavez-140] and [Henrique Capriles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henrique_Capriles)[^cite_note-capriles-141] (who denied the accusations)[^cite_note-142] for a smaller figure, with him being denounced before the Prosecutor's Office by politician [Luis Tellerías](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luis_Teller%C3%ADas?action=edit\&redlink=1) because of these events.[^cite_note-143]

#### Freezing of Swiss accounts linked to a relative of Haiman El Troudi

In July 2017, prosecutors in [Switzerland](26748.md) froze 42 million dollars from eight bank accounts that received transfers from offshore companies linked to Odebrecht in the name of Elita Zacarías, mother-in-law of former minister [Haiman El Troudi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haiman_El_Troudi?action=edit\&redlink=1), and both Elita del Valle and El Troudi's wife, María Baptista, were summoned as defendants for allegedly being related to the corruption case. The court in charge of the case in Venezuela exempted the family from responsibility, disregarding the investigations carried out in Switzerland.[^cite_note-144][^cite_note-145] During his administration, El Troudi used supplementary documents to modify the amounts in contracts for works such as [Caracas Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caracas_Metro) Guarenas-Guatire, Line 5 of the [Caracas Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caracas_Metro), Line 2 of the [Los Teques Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Teques_Metro) and [Metrocable (Caracas)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metrocable_\(Caracas\)) Mariche-La Dolorita.[^cite_note-146]

#### Complaint against Maduro by the Prosecutor's Office

On 19 August 2017 attorney general [Luisa Ortega Díaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luisa_Ortega_D%C3%ADaz) made a complaint against [Nicolás Maduro](7161476.md) through an audio released by the prosecutor's office of [Puebla](413436.md) in the Odebrecht bribery case; around 98 million dollars would have been received between 2005 and 2015. This led her to flee the country, leaving from the Paraguaná Peninsula on a yacht toward Aruba and then to Colombia. The attorney general and another 64 Venezuelan prosecutors were prohibited from leaving the country.[^cite_note-147][^cite_note-148]

In October 2017 attorney general Ortega Díaz made public a video where Euzenando Prazeres de Azevedo, the former director of Odebrecht in Venezuela, declared before the Attorney General's Office of Brazil that Maduro had received US\$35 million to finance his presidential campaign in 2013. In the [recording, Euzenando Prazeres de Azevedo says that Américo Mata](https://www.google.com/search?q=quien+es+Euzenando+Acevedo+en+el+caso+Odrebecht+en+Venezuela\&sca_esv=f0917c4e94fcca3f\&hl=es\&sxsrf=AE3TifPiA2n_XrBDC-C0zQXUi0sweaS3Iw%3A1759930541152\&ei=rWjmaLz9CJ-RwbkPn8mimQE\&ved=0ahUKEwj8h_CV3JSQAxWfSDABHZ-kKBMQ4dUDCBA\&oq=quien+es+Euzenando+Acevedo+en+el+caso+Odrebecht+en+Venezuela\&gs_lp=Egxnd3Mtd2l6LXNlcnAiPHF1aWVuIGVzIEV1emVuYW5kbyBBY2V2ZWRvIGVuIGVsIGNhc28gT2RyZWJlY2h0IGVuIFZlbmV6dWVsYTIIEAAYgAQYogQyCBAAGIAEGKIEMggQABiABBiiBDIIEAAYgAQYogRI9Z55UOgIWIEScAF4AJABAJgBmwigAeEPqgEFNi0xLjG4AQzIAQD4AQGYAgKgAr0RmAMA4gMFEgExIECIBgGSBwM3LTKgB78KsgcDNy0yuAe9EcIHBzQtMS4wLjHIB7oB\&sclient=gws-wiz-serp#fpstate=ive\&vld=cid\:ad385435,vid\:O9L4Ecbfgic,st\:0), whom he identified as a representative of Maduro, contacted him to request money for Maduro's campaign.[^cite_note-149]

#### Trial of Nicolás Maduro



Approval of the judicial process against Nicolás Maduro by the National Assembly on 17 April 2018.



Final act of 23 August where Nicolás Maduro is sentenced to 18 years in prison.

On 22 February 2018, the magistrates of the Full Chamber of the [Supreme Tribunal of Justice of Venezuela in exile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Tribunal_of_Justice_of_Venezuela_in_exile) admitted the request for a preliminary hearing presented by attorney general [Luisa Ortega Díaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luisa_Ortega_D%C3%ADaz) against [Nicolás Maduro](7161476.md) for the crimes of corruption and money laundering related to the payment of bribes allegedly made by the company [Odebrecht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odebrecht_Organization?action=edit\&redlink=1) to several Venezuelan politicians with the purpose of obtaining contracts in major public works. Luisa Ortega stated that in an investigation carried out by her office it was possible to verify that in 2012 the presidential campaign of [Hugo Chávez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez) was paid for by Odebrecht and the money was received by Maduro, who served as foreign minister.[^cite_note-150]

On 28 March the Supreme Tribunal notified Nicolás Maduro to appear on 3 April at a preliminary hearing for corruption accusations related to the Odebrecht case.[^cite_note-151] In the face of his nonappearance, the Supreme Tribunal proceeded to appoint trial lawyer Andrés Felipe Lindo as public defender, in order to guarantee the right to defense.[^cite_note-152] On 9 April the tribunal admitted the preliminary hearing against Maduro requested by the attorney general, ordering preventive detention against the president and requesting a red notice from [Interpol](14338129.md) against him.[^cite_note-153]

On 17 April the National Assembly approved with 105 votes in favor, from the opposition bloc, and two against, Juan Marín ([PSUV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Socialist_Party_of_Venezuela)) and [Ilenia Medina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilenia_Medina?action=edit\&redlink=1) ([PPT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatherland_for_All)) from the ruling party, the continuation of the trial against president Nicolás Maduro for corruption in response to the request submitted by the magistrates of the Supreme Tribunal. The agreement voted on by the deputies established declaring that sufficient merits exist to continue the judicial process and continue with the investigations carried out in the National Assembly in accordance with Article 187 of the constitution for acts of corruption that could derive from Maduro. The vote was carried out nominally and the leadership of the assembly decided to approve the trial with a simple majority; that is, 84 votes, based on Article 89 of the Parliament's Internal Rules and Debates Regulations. The leader of the bloc of the [Democratic Unity Roundtable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Unity_Roundtable) (MUD), [Juan Guaidó](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Guaid%C3%B3), read the report in which president Nicolás Maduro approved 30 billion dollars for the execution of works in charge of Odebrecht that were halted or unfinished. By orders of colonel [Bladimir Lugo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bladimir_Lugo) of the [Bolivarian National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolivarian_National_Guard) (GNB), the media were unable to enter the [Legislative Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palacio_Federal_Legislativo); the colonel kept journalists waiting for more than one hour at the San Francisco corner to finally decide that they would not have access.[^cite_note-154]

On 3 May 2018 the Supreme Tribunal declared Maduro suspended as president and ordered his disqualification from any other public office.[^cite_note-155][^cite_note-156] OAS secretary general Luis Almagro recognized Maduro's disqualification and suspension as president.[^cite_note-157] The political party [Vente Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vente_Venezuela) published a statement supporting the tribunal's decision, stating that "it ratifies that Maduro cannot be a candidate in any election" and that with this decision it corresponds to the National Assembly to begin a new process "to occupy that vacuum and advance in the restoration of democratic order in the country".[^cite_note-158] The suspension from office and detention order was also supported by the Federation of Law Students of Venezuela (Fedeve), which rejected "the persecution and abuses carried out by the repressive dictatorship against the magistrates of the Supreme Tribunal of Justice (TSJ) in exile, and consequently against their relatives in Venezuela" in a statement, and its members demanded that the competent international organizations take pertinent actions in response to such acts.[^cite_note-159]

On 15 August, the Supreme Tribunal sentenced Nicolás Maduro to 18 years and 3 months in prison in [Ramo Verde prison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramo_Verde_prison?action=edit\&redlink=1) for the corruption crimes imputed to him, in addition to ordering him to pay 35 billion dollars for the damage to the country's public patrimony.[^cite_note-160] The sentence has not yet been carried out, since the [Maduro government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Executive_Power_\(Venezuela\)?action=edit\&redlink=1) only recognizes the [Supreme Tribunal of Justice of Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Tribunal_of_Justice_of_Venezuela).[^cite_note-161]

## Foreign policy

Main article: [Foreign relations of Venezuela](32461.md)

The foreign policy of Maduro's second term was characterized by the continuation of alliances with Cuba, Nicaragua, Russia, China, Iran, and Turkey,[^cite_note-\:9-162] alongside the severance of diplomatic relations with [Argentina](18951905.md), [Bolivia](3462.md) (restored in 2020),[^cite_note-\:5-8] [Brazil](3383.md) (restored in 2022),[^cite_note-\:8-163] [Colombia](5222.md) (restored in 2022),[^cite_note-\:6-10] [El Salvador](9356.md), the [United States](3434750.md),[^cite_note-\:7-11] [Guatemala](17238567.md)[^cite_note-\:2-12] and [Paraguay](23420.md).

### Americas

#### Argentina



[Cristina Fernández de Kirchner](1644780.md) and Nicolás Maduro at Chávez's funeral.

In December 2023 it became known that the [government of Javier Milei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Javier_Milei) would not appoint an ambassador to Venezuela. Milei did not invite Maduro to his inauguration.[^cite_note-\:83-164]

After a Venezuelan [Boeing 747](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_747) aircraft in Buenos Aires was seized and handed over to the United States in 2024 because, according to the United States Department of Justice, it had been sold by the Iranian company Mahan Air, which was sanctioned by U.S. authorities, Maduro declared on television on 15 February 2024 that "the bandit Milei stole Venezuela's plane" and that "he pretends to be crazy or he is crazy or both at the same time".[^cite_note-\:1000-165]

On 18 July 2024 Nicolás Maduro called Javier Milei a "bastard" and accused him of being involved in an alleged plot to suspend the [presidential elections](60825396.md).[^cite_note-\:4-166] One day later, the Argentine Foreign Ministry informed the [International Criminal Court](14880.md) (ICC) of Argentina's return to the trial concerning [human rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_during_the_Bolivarian_Revolution_in_Venezuela?action=edit\&redlink=1) in the country due to the "deterioration of the political and human rights situation in Venezuela".[^cite_note-167] On 22 July Argentine presidential spokesman [Manuel Adorni](76203355.md) declared: "Maduro is a figure who has become, or perhaps always was, a dictator".[^cite_note-\:4-166]

On 23 July 2024 former Peronist president [Alberto Fernández](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberto_Fern%C3%A1ndez) confirmed his participation as an electoral observer, declaring that he would meet with opposition leaders to "listen to their concerns" and reiterated that "if (Nicolás Maduro) is defeated, what he has to do is accept it".[^cite_note-168] The following day Fernández confirmed that his invitation had been cancelled because his statements "caused discomfort and generated doubts about my impartiality"; Fernández protested the decision and criticised that his opinion coincided with Brazilian president [*Lula* Da Silva](99328.md)'s criticism of Maduro's statements about a possible "bloodbath" following an eventual electoral defeat.[^cite_note-\:12-169] Chilean president [Gabriel Boric](47329497.md) supported him.[^cite_note-\:13-170]

Following the presidential elections, the Argentine Foreign Ministry issued a statement that day together with the foreign ministers of Costa Rica, Ecuador, Panama, Paraguay, Peru and Uruguay, requesting guarantees that the government would respect the election results.[^cite_note-\:15-171] President Milei declared "Dictator Maduro, out", establishing that "Argentina is not going to recognise another fraud" and asking "that the Armed Forces this time defend democracy and the popular will".[^cite_note-172] Former president [Mauricio Macri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mauricio_Macri) said "Maduro must leave power. Now Venezuela's Armed Forces have the opportunity to stand on the right side of history".[^cite_note-173]

[Pedro Urruchurtu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_Urruchurtu?action=edit\&redlink=1), a political asylum seeker in the Argentine embassy in Venezuela, denounced a [siege by hooded individuals at the ambassador's residence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_the_Argentine_ambassador's_residence_in_Venezuela?action=edit\&redlink=1) that day, where five more dissidents from Maduro's government were taking refuge.[^cite_note-\:25-174] On the same day, in Argentina, Venezuelan ambassador [Stella Lugo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stella_Lugo?action=edit\&redlink=1) accused Security Minister [Patricia Bullrich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patricia_Bullrich) of being "interventionist" following her support for Venezuelan migrants who voted and awaited the results of the presidential elections on 28 July.[^cite_note-175]

On 29 July Maduro's government ordered the expulsion from the country of the diplomatic staff of the Argentine Embassy in Venezuela, accusing it of interventionism following statements by Milei's government regarding its refusal to recognise the results announced by [Elvis Amoroso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elvis_Amoroso) on behalf of the [CNE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Electoral_Council_\(Venezuela\)) after the opposition denounced fraud;[^cite_note-\:20-176] it later cut off electricity and water at the embassy.[^cite_note-\:25-174] The Argentine Foreign Ministry condemned the "harassment" of its embassy.[^cite_note-\:25-174] The [Socialist International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_International) condemned the expulsion of the Argentine diplomatic corps[^cite_note-\:21-177] and President Lula reiterated to Maduro that guaranteeing the security of the embassy was his responsibility.[^cite_note-\:25-174] The foreign ministers of Argentina and Brazil agreed that Lula's government would take charge of Argentina's interests in Venezuela, including its embassy, beginning on 1 August.[^cite_note-\:28-178]

Following the ruling of the [TSJ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Tribunal_of_Justice_of_Venezuela) on 22 August endorsing Nicolás Maduro's victory for his third term, the governments of Argentina, Chile, Costa Rica, Ecuador, the United States, Guatemala, Panama, Paraguay, Peru, the Dominican Republic and Uruguay issued a statement the following day rejecting the ruling.[^cite_note-\:29-179] That day a Venezuelan employee of the Argentine embassy was detained by the [Sebin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolivarian_Intelligence_Service) and released the following day after diplomatic efforts.[^cite_note-180]

Nicolás Maduro's government stated in a communiqué that Venezuela had been "forced" to make the decision to revoke Brazil's permission because of alleged "[terrorist](30636.md) activities" and "attempts at [assassination](2963.md) against Nicolás Maduro".[^cite_note-181]

#### Bolivia



[Evo Morales](284163.md), Nicolás Maduro and [Rafael Correa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafael_Correa).

President [Jeanine Áñez](62310179.md) announced the severance of relations with Venezuela in 2019.[^cite_note-\:93-182] Relations were restored the following year after the arrival of [Luis Arce](38820569.md) to the presidency.[^cite_note-\:52-183]

Former president [Jorge Quiroga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_Quiroga_Ram%C3%ADrez), who was going to serve as an international observer in the [2024 presidential elections](60825396.md), was prevented from travelling to the country from [Panama](22997.md) on 26 July of that year after Venezuelan airspace was blocked to his plane, which carried other Latin American former presidents and ordinary passengers.[^cite_note-\:14-184] The [government of Luis Arce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Luis_Arce?action=edit\&redlink=1) recognised Nicolás Maduro as the winner of the elections and congratulated him.[^cite_note-185]

#### Brazil



Brazilian President [Dilma Rousseff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dilma_Rousseff) receiving a photograph of [Hugo Chávez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez) from Maduro, 9 May 2013



Maduro with Brazilian President [Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva](99328.md) in Brasília, Brazil, 29 May 2023

Venezuela-[Brazil](3383.md) relations have swung from amicable during the leftist administrations of [Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva](99328.md) (2003–2010; 2023–present) and [Dilma Rousseff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dilma_Rousseff) (2010–2016), to antagonistic during the right-wing administrations of [Michel Temer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michel_Temer) (2016–2019) and [Jair Bolsonaro](46603496.md) (2019–2023).[^cite_note-186]

On 14 January 2019, days after Brazil recognized Venezuelan opposition leader [Juan Guaidó](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Guaid%C3%B3) as the country's interim president, Maduro called Brazilian President Jair Bolsonaro "a [Hitler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hitler) of the modern era".[^cite_note-187]

#### Chile

Between 2018 and 2023 Chile did not appoint any ambassador to Venezuela, until president Gabriel Boric appointed [Jaime Gazmuri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaime_Gazmuri) in May 2023. Boric asked the United States and the European Union to lift the [economic sanctions against Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_sanctions_during_the_Venezuelan_crisis). Boric opposed Brazilian president Lula Da Silva's statement in May 2023 that "authoritarianism (in Venezuela) is a narrative construction", saying "I have had the opportunity to see (that reality) in the eyes and pain of hundreds of thousands of Venezuelans who today are in our homeland and who also demand a firm and clear position".[^cite_note-188][^cite_note-189]

Following the kidnapping and murder of former Venezuelan serviceman [Ronald Ojeda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ronald_Ojeda), a political dissident granted [asylum](1621432.md) in Chile, in February 2024, relations between both countries deteriorated. Chilean prosecutor Héctor Barros determined in April that so far the only possible motive was political due to the victim's profile as an active [dissident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opposition_to_Nicol%C3%A1s_Maduro?action=edit\&redlink=1). He declared Walter Rodríguez, whose fingerprint was found on Ojeda's mobile phone, and Maickel Villegas, who took his family out of Chile one month before the former lieutenant's murder, as suspects. In June, Venezuelan prosecutor Tarek William Saab described the crime as a "[false flag operation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/False_flag_operation)", remarks that Foreign Minister [Alberto van Klaveren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberto_van_Klaveren) described as "unacceptable".[^cite_note-190]

Following Nicolás Maduro's statements on the eve of the [presidential elections](60825396.md) regarding the possibility of a "bloodbath" in the country if he lost the elections, [Gabriel Boric](47329497.md) declared: "One cannot threaten bloodbaths under any circumstances", calling for respect for the popular will.[^cite_note-\:13-170] After the elections, Boric did not recognise the electoral results declaring Maduro the winner, considering them "difficult to believe", and requested transparency regarding the tally sheets and electoral process.[^cite_note-\:17-191] On 29 July Maduro's government ordered the expulsion from the country of the diplomatic staff of the Chilean Embassy in Venezuela, accusing it of interventionism following statements by Boric's government.[^cite_note-\:20-176] The [Socialist International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_International) condemned the expulsion of the Chilean diplomatic corps.[^cite_note-\:21-177] Following the ruling of the [TSJ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Tribunal_of_Justice_of_Venezuela) on 22 August endorsing Nicolás Maduro's victory for his third term, Boric declared that "Today Venezuela's TSJ finishes consolidating the fraud. Maduro's regime obviously welcomes its ruling, which will be marked by infamy" and clarified that "Venezuela's dictatorship is not the left".[^cite_note-192]

#### Colombia

##### Government of Juan Manuel Santos

In 2014 Nicolás Maduro's government accused Juan Manuel Santos' government of being behind the [protests of that year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Venezuelan_protests).[^cite_note-\:33-193]



An elderly Colombian refugee transported by the [National Police of Colombia](7278792.md) across the [Táchira River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C3%A1chira_River) from Venezuela to Colombia.

In 2015 the government responded to the murder of three Venezuelan soldiers on the border with Colombia by establishing a [state of emergency](298608.md), during which 1,012 Colombians were deported and 10 alleged paramilitaries from that country were captured, according to statements by then-governor [José Vielma Mora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Vielma_Mora),[^cite_note-194] with estimates of around 20,000 undocumented Colombians displaced by government policies intended to secure control of the border.[^cite_note-195][^cite_note-196]

In 2018 Nicolás Maduro's government accused Juan Manuel Santos' government of being behind the [attack of that year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Caracas_drone_attack) against him.[^cite_note-\:33-193]

##### Government of Iván Duque

Venezuela and Colombia broke [diplomatic relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colombia-Venezuela_relations) in February 2019 after Colombia recognised [Juan Guaidó](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Guaid%C3%B3) as president.[^cite_note-\:93-182] Venezuela gave Colombian diplomats 24 hours to leave the country.[^cite_note-197]

##### Government of Gustavo Petro

Relations between both countries were restored in 2022 after the arrival of [Gustavo Petro](13391962.md) to the presidency.[^cite_note-\:62-198]

In March 2024 Colombia's Foreign Ministry expressed "concern" regarding the "difficulties faced by major sectors of the [opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opposition_to_Nicol%C3%A1s_Maduro?action=edit\&redlink=1)" in nominating their candidates, in reference to [Corina Yoris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corina_Yoris).[^cite_note-199] Foreign Minister [Yván Gil](76565361.md) responded: "Driven by the need to satisfy the wishes of the [U.S. State Department](31975.md), Colombia's Foreign Ministry takes a false step and commits a gross act of interference in matters that concern only Venezuelans".[^cite_note-\:0-200]

On 2 April 2024 Petro described the disqualification of [María Corina Machado](5791389.md) as a "democratic coup".[^cite_note-\:0-200] On 10 April Maduro and Petro met.[^cite_note-201]

Petro proposed an agreement on democratic guarantees between the government and the opposition; Colombian Foreign Minister Luis Gilberto Murillo expressed the [Colombian government's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Gustavo_Petro) intention to ensure that Venezuela could have a peaceful [transition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_transition?action=edit\&redlink=1) after the elections.[^cite_note-202] Former vice president [Diosdado Cabello](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diosdado_Cabello) responded: "Who sent you to say that? Your president of Colombia or your president of the United States? Who do you work for? Who gives you the right to speak about transition in Venezuela?", stating that the only transition that would come to Venezuela would be "the transition to [socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_state)".[^cite_note-203] Colombia did not officially respond to Cabello; however, when questioned on the matter, Murillo stated: "To foolish words, deaf ears".[^cite_note-204]

Former Colombian vice president [Marta Lucía Ramírez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marta_Luc%C3%ADa_Ram%C3%ADrez), who was going to serve as an international observer in the [2024 presidential elections](60825396.md), was prevented from travelling to the country from [Panama](22997.md) on 26 July of that year after Venezuelan airspace was blocked to her plane, which also carried other Latin American former presidents and ordinary passengers, causing her and the others to disembark so that the aircraft could continue without restrictions.[^cite_note-\:14-184]

Foreign Minister [Luis Gilberto Murillo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luis_Gilberto_Murillo) declared that "We call for the total counting of votes, their verification and an independent audit to proceed as soon as possible", requesting that "any doubts regarding the results be dispelled". Former president [Andrés Pastrana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9s_Pastrana) declared: "In Venezuela there was not electoral fraud, but rather a coup d'état through the disregard of the popular will!".[^cite_note-\:18-205]

#### Costa Rica

Former Costa Rican president [Miguel Ángel Rodríguez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_%C3%81ngel_Rodr%C3%ADguez_Echeverr%C3%ADa), who was going to serve as an international observer in the [2024 presidential elections](60825396.md), was prevented from travelling to the country from [Panama](22997.md) on 26 July of that year after Venezuelan airspace was blocked to his plane, which carried other Latin American former presidents and ordinary passengers, causing him and the others to disembark so that the aircraft could continue without restrictions.[^cite_note-\:14-184]

Following the presidential elections, Costa Rica's Foreign Ministry issued a statement together with the foreign ministers of Argentina, Ecuador, Panama, Paraguay, Peru and Uruguay, requesting guarantees that the government would respect the election results.[^cite_note-\:15-171]

Following the Venezuelan elections, president [Rodrigo Chaves](70007255.md) declared that "The government of Costa Rica categorically repudiates the proclamation of Nicolás Maduro as president of the Bolivarian Republic of Venezuela, which we consider fraudulent". On 29 July Maduro's government ordered the expulsion from the country of the diplomatic staff of the Costa Rican Embassy in Venezuela.[^cite_note-\:20-176] The [Socialist International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_International) condemned the expulsion of the Costa Rican diplomatic corps.[^cite_note-\:21-177] On 31 July Costa Rica offered political asylum to [Edmundo González Urrutia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmundo_Gonz%C3%A1lez_Urrutia) and [María Corina Machado](5791389.md), leaders of the opposition to the government,[^cite_note-206] an option they appreciated but declined.

On 2 August 2024 Chaves' government recognised [Edmundo González Urrutia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmundo_Gonz%C3%A1lez_Urrutia) as president-elect. Following the ruling of the [TSJ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Tribunal_of_Justice_of_Venezuela) on 22 August endorsing Nicolás Maduro's victory for his third term, the governments of Argentina, Chile, Costa Rica, Ecuador, the United States, Guatemala, Panama, Paraguay, Peru, the Dominican Republic and Uruguay issued a statement the following day rejecting the ruling.[^cite_note-\:29-179]

#### Ecuador

##### Government of Lenín Moreno

In 2018, after [Jorge Rodríguez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_Rodr%C3%ADguez_G%C3%B3mez) called president [Lenín Moreno](8187373.md) a "liar" and accused him of "inflating" the figures of Venezuelan emigrants, Venezuelan ambassador Carol Delgado was given 72 hours to leave the country, which was reciprocated in Venezuela with the expulsion of Ecuador's chargé d'affaires.[^cite_note-\:222-207][^cite_note-208]

In 2019 Lenín Moreno accused former president [Rafael Correa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafael_Correa) and Nicolás Maduro of conspiring against him and of being behind the social unrest in Ecuador, describing it as an "attempted coup d'état".[^cite_note-209] Following this, Correa clarified that he had been in Venezuela working as a consultant for Maduro's government.[^cite_note-210]

##### Government of Daniel Noboa

In April 2024, following the [raid by Daniel Noboa's government on the Mexican embassy in Ecuador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raid_on_the_Mexican_embassy_in_Ecuador?action=edit\&redlink=1), Maduro's government announced the closure of Venezuela's embassy in Ecuador as well as its consulates in the country.[^cite_note-211] On 18 July 2024 Maduro accused Noboa of being behind an alleged plan to suspend the [presidential elections in Venezuela](60825396.md).[^cite_note-\:4-166]

Following the Venezuelan presidential elections, Ecuador's Foreign Ministry issued a statement together with the foreign ministers of Argentina, Costa Rica, Panama, Paraguay, Peru and Uruguay, requesting guarantees that the government would respect the election results.[^cite_note-\:15-171]

Ecuador's Foreign Ministry rejected the results declaring Maduro the winner because of their "lack of transparency"; for the country, the "lack of guarantees" in the vote-counting process "delegitimises and taints the election results". President Noboa declared that he had asked Foreign Minister [Gabriela Sommerfeld](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabriela_Sommerfeld) to convene the Permanent Council of the [OAS](22788.md) to address the "delicate situation Venezuela is experiencing", adding that this was "the danger of dictatorship".[^cite_note-212] On 2 August Noboa's government recognised [Edmundo González Urrutia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmundo_Gonz%C3%A1lez_Urrutia) as president-elect. Following the ruling of the [TSJ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Tribunal_of_Justice_of_Venezuela) on 22 August endorsing Nicolás Maduro's victory for his third term, the governments of Argentina, Chile, Costa Rica, Ecuador, the United States, Guatemala, Panama, Paraguay, Peru, the Dominican Republic and Uruguay issued a statement the following day rejecting the ruling.[^cite_note-\:29-179]

#### El Salvador



Nicolás Maduro and [Salvador Sánchez Cerén](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvador_S%C3%A1nchez_Cer%C3%A9n) in 2019.

Nicolás Maduro expelled the Salvadoran diplomatic corps, which was reciprocated by president [Nayib Bukele](46452145.md) with the expulsion of all representatives of the Venezuelan embassy in the country.[^cite_note-\:222-207]

#### Guatemala

President [Alejandro Giammattei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alejandro_Giammattei) closed Guatemala's embassy in Caracas in 2020 and broke diplomatic relations with Nicolás Maduro's government.[^cite_note-\:93-182][^cite_note-\:222-207]

Following the results of the 2024 presidential elections, president [Bernardo Arévalo](67960960.md) wrote on the social network [X](9988187.md) that "Venezuela deserves transparent, accurate electoral results in accordance with the will of its people" and that "we received the results announced by the CNE with many doubts. Therefore, the reports of the electoral observation missions are indispensable and, today more than ever, they must defend the votes of Venezuelans". Following the ruling of the [TSJ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Tribunal_of_Justice_of_Venezuela) on 22 August endorsing Nicolás Maduro's victory for his third term, the governments of Argentina, Chile, Costa Rica, Ecuador, the United States, Guatemala, Panama, Paraguay, Peru, the Dominican Republic and Uruguay issued a statement the following day rejecting the ruling.[^cite_note-\:29-179]

#### Guyana

Main article: [Guyana–Venezuela crisis (2023–2024)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guyana%E2%80%93Venezuela_crisis_\(2023%E2%80%932024\))

#### Honduras

In 2019 [Honduras](13394.md) withdrew its chargé d'affaires in Caracas and recognised [Juan Guaidó](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Guaid%C3%B3) as president.[^cite_note-\:93-182] Both countries broke [relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honduras-Venezuela_relations?action=edit\&redlink=1) until 2022, when they were resumed.[^cite_note-\:102-213] The [government of Xiomara Castro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Xiomara_Castro?action=edit\&redlink=1) recognised Nicolás Maduro as the winner of the 2024 presidential elections, congratulating him.[^cite_note-\:31-214]

#### Mexico

##### Government of Enrique Peña Nieto

After Maduro's election as president in April 2013, the government of [Enrique Peña Nieto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enrique_Pe%C3%B1a_Nieto) further emphasised the close ties between both countries and the willingness to overcome differences regarding political structures. However, in January 2015 Maduro accused former Mexican president [Felipe Calderón Hinojosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Felipe_Calder%C3%B3n_Hinojosa) (Peña Nieto's predecessor) of conspiring with the Venezuelan opposition to overthrow his government.[^cite_note-215] In 2017 Maduro expressed support for Peña Nieto during the [diplomatic crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017%E2%80%932020_Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_diplomatic_crisis?action=edit\&redlink=1) with the government of [Donald Trump](4848272.md) regarding issues such as the [border wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_barrier) and the renegotiation of [NAFTA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NAFTA).[^cite_note-216]

##### Government of Andrés Manuel López Obrador

Following [Andrés Manuel López Obrador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9s_Manuel_L%C3%B3pez_Obrador)'s victory in July 2018, president Maduro travelled to Mexico to attend the inauguration in December of that same year.[^cite_note-217] On 23 January 2019 López Obrador's government reiterated Mexico's traditional stance of non-intervention in the affairs of other countries, supporting Maduro's government by refusing to recognise [Juan Guaidó](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Guaid%C3%B3) as president of Venezuela.[^cite_note-218]

Former president [Vicente Fox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vicente_Fox), who was going to serve as an international observer in the [Venezuelan presidential elections](60825396.md), was prevented from travelling to the country from [Panama](22997.md) on 26 July of that year after Venezuelan airspace was blocked to his plane, which carried other Latin American former presidents and ordinary passengers, causing him and the others to disembark so that the aircraft could continue without restrictions.[^cite_note-\:14-184]

#### Panama

##### Government of Ricardo Martinelli

In March 2014 Nicolás Maduro announced the severance of [diplomatic relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panama-Venezuela_relations) with [Panama](22997.md) after the latter country requested that the Permanent Council of the [Organization of American States](22788.md) (OAS) convene a meeting of foreign ministers regarding the situation in Venezuela.[^cite_note-219]

##### Government of Juan Carlos Varela

Panama designated Maduro and other Venezuelan officials as "high-risk" subjects for money laundering. [Nicolás Maduro](7161476.md) responded on 5 April 2018 by declaring sanctions against Panamanian companies, as well as against the [president of Panama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Panama), [Juan Carlos Varela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Carlos_Varela), and other businessmen. The crisis ended on 26 April when Maduro announced that he had spoken by telephone with Varela and they agreed on the return of ambassadors and the restoration of air communication between both countries.[^cite_note-BBC-220][^cite_note-\:323-221]

##### Government of José Raúl Mulino

On 26 July 2024 a [Copa Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copa_Airlines) aircraft was prevented from travelling from [Panama](22997.md) to Venezuela after Venezuelan airspace was blocked because of the presence of former presidents [Mireya Moscoso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mireya_Moscoso) (Panama), Vicente Fox (Mexico), Miguel Ángel Rodríguez (Costa Rica) and Jorge Quiroga (Bolivia), and former Colombian vice president [Marta Lucía Ramírez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marta_Luc%C3%ADa_Ram%C3%ADrez), who were going to serve as international observers in the [2024 presidential elections](60825396.md). The politicians disembarked so that the aircraft could continue without restrictions.[^cite_note-\:14-184]
